I just know I always end up on my feet.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to get up and start pacing, do you experience an uncomfortable feeling inside—like a restless tension or an urge that you have to act on—or does it feel more like your mind is just wandering and you naturally want to move around?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know how to describe the feeling inside. I haven't really thought about whether it's a restless tension or just my mind wandering. I just know that I can't stay seated. I haven't really noticed what exactly is going on in my head or my body right before I stand up. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, do you feel something physically uncomfortable inside your body—like a tension or an itch in your muscles or a restless feeling that makes you need to move—or does it feel more like your thoughts are just pulling you away from sitting?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to break it down like that. It's not like I feel an itch or a specific tension in my muscles... and I wouldn't say my thoughts are pulling me away, either. It's just a general feeling of needing to not be sitting anymore. I'm sorry, I know you're trying to get a specific answer, but I just haven't analyzed it that closely. I just know I end up walking around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with not being able to stay seated been going on? Has it always been like this for you, or did it start at a particular time?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a bit antsy, I guess, but it's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Ever since then, it just seems a lot harder to stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you moved out of your apartment in Chicago eight months ago, was that move stressful or difficult for you? What was going on at that time?\n\nPatient: I mean, moving is always a bit of a hassle, just dealing with boxes and logistics. But... no, it wasn't particularly difficult or stressful. Nothing major was going on in my life. My lease was just up, so I found a new place. I really don't know why it got so much harder to sit still around that time. It just sort of... happened, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like working on a case as a private investigator—are you able to sit still and concentrate, or do you find yourself getting up and pacing even then?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed if there's a difference, to be honest. When I'm deep into a case... well, I'm not entirely sure if I stay seated longer just because it's interesting. I haven't really paid attention to it like that. I just know I'm up and down a lot during the day, pretty much no matter what I'm working on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to get up and pace, if you tried very hard to stay seated—say, if your job depended on it—do you think you could force yourself to stay in the chair, or does it feel like something you literally cannot control no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. I guess I've never been in a situation where I absolutely, 100 percent couldn't get up. I just feel this... this really strong urge to move, and it's so uncomfortable to fight it. I don't know if I could force myself to stay seated if my job depended on it. I just haven't tried to push it that far.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. sessions, even book club—and people are starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet things that used to help me, like yoga or reading at book club, now turn into me whispering comments, rustling papers, or finding some excuse to move around instead of just being still.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don't have field work, I still move from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to running errands like I'm chasing a lead, and I have a hard time just being off duty.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I catch myself talking through every angle of a case or a client meeting until I've basically filled all the space, even when the other person is giving me clear signs they're done.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: If I hear junior detectives discussing a lead or a neighbor talking in the hallway, I jump in with questions or advice before I've been asked, and afterward I realize I barged into someone else's conversation.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My grade-school report cards always said I was bright but couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn, and my mother still jokes that I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: History]\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like working on a case as a private investigator—are you able to sit still and concentrate, or do you find yourself getting up and pacing even then?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed if there's a difference, to be honest. I don't really know. I guess I've never been in a situation where I absolutely, 100 percent couldn't get up. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it's an inner agitation or just my body wanting to be active. It's hard to separate the two. I just... I just know that sitting still starts to feel wrong after a while, so I get up. I really don't know how else to explain it. I feel like you're asking the same question again, and I just don't have a clear answer for it. I haven't analyzed it that deeply. I just sit, I get the urge to move, and I get up. I don't know if it's an inner bother or just my body wanting motion. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely paralyzed from the neck down but still awake and aware, would you feel a strong inner discomfort or agitation that makes you want to move—even though you couldn't—or would you just feel fine sitting still since your body wouldn't be able to move anyway?\n\nPatient: I... wow, that's an extreme example. I honestly don't know how I'd feel in that situation. I've never been paralyzed, so I can't really guess if I'd feel agitated inside or if I'd just be fine. I mean, my brain would probably still be going a million miles an hour, but as for the physical urge to move? I just don't know. I can't really imagine what that would feel like.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get that urge to stand up and pace, if you were forced to stay seated for 10 minutes, would you feel anxious, irritable, or emotionally distressed—like something is wrong or bothering you—or would you just be bored or restless without that emotional upset?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I think... if I had to sit there for ten minutes and absolutely couldn't get up, I’d probably just feel really restless. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely paralyzed from the neck down but still awake and aware, would you feel a strong inner discomfort or agitation that makes you want to move—even though you couldn't—or would you just feel fine sitting still since your body wouldn't be able to move anyway?\n\nPatient: I... wow, that's an extreme example. I honestly don't know how I'd feel in that situation. I've never been paralyzed, so I can't really guess if I'd feel agitated inside or if I'd just be fine. I mean, my brain would probably still be going a million miles an hour, but as for the physical urge to move? I just don't know. I can't really imagine what that would feel like.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, if you were forced to stay seated for 10 minutes, would you feel anxious, irritable, or emotionally distressed—like something is wrong or bothering you—or would you just be bored or restless without that emotional upset?\n\nPatient: I think... if I had to sit there for ten minutes and absolutely couldn't get up, I’d probably just feel really restless. Like, my leg would start bouncing, I'd be shifting around in the chair, maybe tapping my fingers. I don't think I'd feel anxious or emotionally upset, like something was terribly wrong. I'd just be really antsy and bored, waiting for the ten minutes to be up so I could finally move.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're sitting and get that urge to move, if you compare it to times when you're bored or waiting in line, is the feeling the same kind of restlessness, or does this urge to move feel like something different—something more internal or harder to ignore?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it's just the same kind of restlessness. Like if I'm stuck waiting in line at the grocery store, I'm shifting my weight, looking around, maybe checking my phone, just wanting things to move along. When I'm sitting and get the urge to get up, it feels pretty much like that. It doesn't feel like some deep, internal... thing that's totally different. It's just that same basic feeling of wanting to be doing something else, or just wanting to be in motion.

Like if I'm stuck waiting in line at the grocery store, I'm shifting my weight, looking around, maybe checking my phone, just wanting things to move along. When I'm sitting and get the urge to get up, it feels pretty much like that. It doesn't feel like some deep, internal... thing that's totally different. It's just that same basic feeling of wanting to be doing something else, or just wanting to be in motion.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something—like reading a case file or listening to someone speak—do you find your mind wandering or getting distracted easily, or are you able to concentrate on what you're doing even though your body wants to move around?\n\nPatient: I can usually concentrate pretty well. I mean, my body might be doing its own thing—like I said, my leg bouncing or me shifting in my seat—but my mind is on the job. If I'm reading a file or listening to a client, I'm focused on what they're saying or what's on the page. I don't really have a problem with my mind wandering off. It's more just the physical need to be moving while I'm doing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned you can usually concentrate well on your work. But when you're reading case files or listening to someone speak, do you ever find it hard to keep your attention on what they're saying, or do you sometimes miss details because your mind drifted to something else?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

I'll just stand up and start pacing. I just know I always end up on my feet.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to get up and start pacing, do you experience an uncomfortable feeling inside—like a restless tension or an urge that you have to act on—or does it feel more like your mind is just wandering and you naturally want to move around?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know how to describe the feeling inside. I haven't really thought about whether it's a restless tension or just my mind wandering. I just know that I can't stay seated. I haven't really noticed what exactly is going on in my head or my body right before I stand up. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, do you feel something physically uncomfortable inside your body—like a tension or an itch in your muscles or a restless feeling that makes you need to move—or does it feel more like your thoughts are just pulling you away from sitting?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to break it down like that. It's not like I feel an itch or a specific tension in my muscles... and I wouldn't say my thoughts are pulling me away, either. It's just a general feeling of needing to not be sitting anymore. I'm sorry, I know you're trying to get a specific answer, but I just haven't analyzed it that closely. I just know I end up walking around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with not being able to stay seated been going on? It just sort of... happened, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like working on a case as a private investigator—are you able to sit still and concentrate, or do you find yourself getting up and pacing even then?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed if there's a difference, to be honest. When I'm deep into a case... well, I'm not entirely sure if I stay seated longer just because it's interesting. I haven't really paid attention to it like that. I just know I'm up and down a lot during the day, pretty much no matter what I'm working on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to get up and pace, if you tried very hard to stay seated—say, if your job depended on it—do you think you could force yourself to stay in the chair, or does it feel like something you literally cannot control no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. I guess I've never been in a situation where I absolutely, 100 percent couldn't get up. I just feel this... this really strong urge to move, and it's so uncomfortable to fight it. I don't know if I could force myself to stay seated if my job depended on it. I just haven't tried to push it that far.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, would you say there's an uncomfortable feeling inside—like an inner agitation or restlessness—that makes you want to move, or is it more that you just don't feel like sitting and your body naturally wants to be active?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I really don't know how else to explain it. I feel like you're asking the same question again, and I just don't have a clear answer for it. I haven't analyzed it that deeply. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely paralyzed from the neck down but still awake and aware, would you feel a strong inner discomfort or agitation that makes you want to move—even though you couldn't—or would you just feel fine sitting still since your body wouldn't be able to move anyway?\n\nPatient: I... wow, that's an extreme example. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. Thank you for coming in today. What brings you to see me?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Nice to meet you. \n\nWell, what brings me in is that I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to stay put. I'm a private investigator, and it happens a lot during client interviews. I'll just stand up and start pacing. It happens outside of work, too, like during tutoring sessions or even at my book club. \n\nPeople are really starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen, and it's becoming an issue for me, so I figured I needed to come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're pacing or standing up during these situations, are you having trouble focusing on what the other person is saying, or is it more that your body just needs to move even though you want to listen?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I guess I haven't paid close attention to exactly why it happens in the moment. \n\nI just know I'll be sitting there, and then... I just feel like I have to get up. I don't really know if I'm losing focus on what they're saying or if my body just refuses to stay in the chair. I just know I always end up on my feet.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that urge to get up and start pacing, do you experience an uncomfortable feeling inside—like a restless tension or an urge that you have to act on—or does it feel more like your mind is just wandering and you naturally want to move around?\n\nPatient: I guess I don't really know how to describe the feeling inside. I haven't really thought about whether it's a restless tension or just my mind wandering. I just know that I can't stay seated. I haven't really noticed what exactly is going on in my head or my body right before I stand up. I just do it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, do you feel something physically uncomfortable inside your body—like a tension or an itch in your muscles or a restless feeling that makes you need to move—or does it feel more like your thoughts are just pulling you away from sitting?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know how to break it down like that. It's not like I feel an itch or a specific tension in my muscles... and I wouldn't say my thoughts are pulling me away, either. It's just a general feeling of needing to not be sitting anymore. I'm sorry, I know you're trying to get a specific answer, but I just haven't analyzed it that closely. I just know I end up walking around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with not being able to stay seated been going on? Has it always been like this for you, or did it start at a particular time?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a bit antsy, I guess, but it's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Ever since then, it just seems a lot harder to stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you moved out of your apartment in Chicago eight months ago, was that move stressful or difficult for you? What was going on at that time?\n\nPatient: I mean, moving is always a bit of a hassle, just dealing with boxes and logistics. But... no, it wasn't particularly difficult or stressful. Nothing major was going on in my life. My lease was just up, so I found a new place. I really don't know why it got so much harder to sit still around that time. It just sort of... happened, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like working on a case as a private investigator—are you able to sit still and concentrate, or do you find yourself getting up and pacing even then?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed if there's a difference, to be honest. When I'm deep into a case... well, I'm not entirely sure if I stay seated longer just because it's interesting. I haven't really paid attention to it like that. I just know I'm up and down a lot during the day, pretty much no matter what I'm working on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to get up and pace, if you tried very hard to stay seated—say, if your job depended on it—do you think you could force yourself to stay in the chair, or does it feel like something you literally cannot control no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. I guess I've never been in a situation where I absolutely, 100 percent couldn't get up. I just feel this... this really strong urge to move, and it's so uncomfortable to fight it. I don't know if I could force myself to stay seated if my job depended on it. I just haven't tried to push it that far.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, would you say there's an uncomfortable feeling inside—like an inner agitation or restlessness—that makes you want to move, or is it more that you just don't feel like sitting and your body naturally wants to be active?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it's an inner agitation or just my body wanting to be active. It's hard to separate the two. I just... I just know that sitting still starts to feel wrong after a while, so I get up. I couldn't really say which one of those it is.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting still and feel that urge to get up, would you describe the feeling as actively uncomfortable or distressing—something that bothers you and makes you want to escape it—or is it more that sitting just doesn't feel natural to you and moving feels better?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to describe it, honestly. It's not like... it's not like a sharp pain or something that actively hurts. But it's also not just that moving feels better. I guess I haven't really thought about whether it's distressing or just unnatural. It's just... I get this feeling, and I have to move. I don't know if I can put it into either of those categories.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting and get that urge to move, does it feel like there's something inside you that's bothering you or making you uncomfortable—like you need to escape that feeling by moving—or does it feel more like your body just naturally wants to be in motion, without any sense that sitting is actually bothering you?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know how else to explain it. I feel like you're asking the same question again, and I just don't have a clear answer for it. I haven't analyzed it that deeply. I just sit, I get the urge to move, and I get up. I don't know if it's an inner bother or just my body wanting motion. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely paralyzed from the neck down but still awake and aware, would you feel a strong inner discomfort or agitation that makes you want to move—even though you couldn't—or would you just feel fine sitting still since your body wouldn't be able to move anyway?\n\nPatient: I... wow, that's an extreme example. I honestly don't know how I'd feel in that situation. I've never been paralyzed, so I can't really guess if I'd feel agitated inside or if I'd just be fine. I mean, my brain would probably still be going a million miles an hour, but as for the physical urge to move? I just don't know. I can't really imagine what that would feel like.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, if you were forced to stay seated for 10 minutes, would you feel anxious, irritable, or emotionally distressed—like something is wrong or bothering you—or would you just be bored or restless without that emotional upset?\n\nPatient: I think... if I had to sit there for ten minutes and absolutely couldn't get up, I’d probably just feel really restless. Like, my leg would start bouncing, I'd be shifting around in the chair, maybe tapping my fingers. I don't think I'd feel anxious or emotionally upset, like something was terribly wrong. I'd just be really antsy and bored, waiting for the ten minutes to be up so I could finally move.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting and get that urge to move, if you compare it to times when you're bored or waiting in line, is the feeling the same kind of restlessness, or does this urge to move feel like something different—something more internal or harder to ignore?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the same kind of restlessness. Like if I'm stuck waiting in line at the grocery store, I'm shifting my weight, looking around, maybe checking my phone, just wanting things to move along. When I'm sitting and get the urge to get up, it feels pretty much like that. It doesn't feel like some deep, internal... thing that's totally different. It's just that same basic feeling of wanting to be doing something else, or just wanting to be in motion.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something—like reading a case file or listening to someone speak—do you find your mind wandering or getting distracted easily, or are you able to concentrate on what you're doing even though your body wants to move around?\n\nPatient: I can usually concentrate pretty well. I mean, my body might be doing its own thing—like I said, my leg bouncing or me shifting in my seat—but my mind is on the job. If I'm reading a file or listening to a client, I'm focused on what they're saying or what's on the page. I don't really have a problem with my mind wandering off. It's more just the physical need to be moving while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can usually concentrate well on your work. But when you're reading case files or listening to someone speak, do you ever find it hard to keep your attention on what they're saying, or do you sometimes miss details because your mind drifted to something else?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, I don't really have an issue with my mind drifting off. In my line of work, missing details isn't really an option anyway. So, no, I don't find it hard to keep my attention on what someone is saying or what I'm reading. I stay focused on the information, it's just my body that wants to keep moving while I do it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at your book club or in tutoring sessions, do you ever have trouble keeping up with what's being discussed because your mind wanders, or do you follow along fine even though your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I follow along fine. It's the same thing as with work—I don't really have trouble keeping up with the discussion. My mind stays on the book or whatever we're talking about in the tutoring session. I might be shifting around in my chair or tapping my foot, but I'm paying attention to what everyone is saying. I don't really space out or miss things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can't really imagine what that would feel like.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, if you were forced to stay seated for 10 minutes, would you feel anxious, irritable, or emotionally distressed—like something is wrong or bothering you—or would you just be bored or restless without that emotional upset?\n\nPatient: I think... if I had to sit there for ten minutes and absolutely couldn't get up, I’d probably just feel really restless. Like, my leg would start bouncing, I'd be shifting around in the chair, maybe tapping my fingers. I don't think I'd feel anxious or emotionally upset, like something was terribly wrong. I'd just be really antsy and bored, waiting for the ten minutes to be up so I could finally move.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting and get that urge to move, if you compare it to times when you're bored or waiting in line, is the feeling the same kind of restlessness, or does this urge to move feel like something different—something more internal or harder to ignore?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the same kind of restlessness. Like if I'm stuck waiting in line at the grocery store, I'm shifting my weight, looking around, maybe checking my phone, just wanting things to move along. When I'm sitting and get the urge to get up, it feels pretty much like that. It doesn't feel like some deep, internal... thing that's totally different. It's just that same basic feeling of wanting to be doing something else, or just wanting to be in motion.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something—like reading a case file or listening to someone speak—do you find your mind wandering or getting distracted easily, or are you able to concentrate on what you're doing even though your body wants to move around?\n\nPatient: I can usually concentrate pretty well. I mean, my body might be doing its own thing—like I said, my leg bouncing or me shifting in my seat—but my mind is on the job. If I'm reading a file or listening to a client, I'm focused on what they're saying or what's on the page. I don't really have a problem with my mind wandering off. It's more just the physical need to be moving while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can usually concentrate well on your work. But when you're reading case files or listening to someone speak, do you ever find it hard to keep your attention on what they're saying, or do you sometimes miss details because your mind drifted to something else?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, I don't really have an issue with my mind drifting off. In my line of work, missing details isn't really an option anyway. So, no, I don't find it hard to keep my attention on what someone is saying or what I'm reading. I stay focused on the information, it's just my body that wants to keep moving while I do it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at your book club or in tutoring sessions, do you ever have trouble keeping up with what's being discussed because your mind wanders, or do you follow along fine even though your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I follow along fine. It's the same thing as with work—I don't really have trouble keeping up with the discussion. My mind stays on the book or whatever we're talking about in the tutoring session. I might be shifting around in my chair or tapping my foot, but I'm paying attention to what everyone is saying. I don't really space out or miss things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's not like I feel an itch or a specific tension in my muscles... and I wouldn't say my thoughts are pulling me away, either. It's just a general feeling of needing to not be sitting anymore. I'm sorry, I know you're trying to get a specific answer, but I just haven't analyzed it that closely. I just know I end up walking around.\n\nDoctor: How long has this problem with not being able to stay seated been going on? Has it always been like this for you, or did it start at a particular time?\n\nPatient: Well, I've always been a bit antsy, I guess, but it's been noticeably worse for about eight months now. That's when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Ever since then, it just seems a lot harder to stay put.\n\nDoctor: When you moved out of your apartment in Chicago eight months ago, was that move stressful or difficult for you? What was going on at that time?\n\nPatient: I mean, moving is always a bit of a hassle, just dealing with boxes and logistics. But... no, it wasn't particularly difficult or stressful. Nothing major was going on in my life. My lease was just up, so I found a new place. I really don't know why it got so much harder to sit still around that time. It just sort of... happened, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something you really enjoy or find interesting—like working on a case as a private investigator—are you able to sit still and concentrate, or do you find yourself getting up and pacing even then?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed if there's a difference, to be honest. When I'm deep into a case... well, I'm not entirely sure if I stay seated longer just because it's interesting. I haven't really paid attention to it like that. I just know I'm up and down a lot during the day, pretty much no matter what I'm working on.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to get up and pace, if you tried very hard to stay seated—say, if your job depended on it—do you think you could force yourself to stay in the chair, or does it feel like something you literally cannot control no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I mean... I don't really know. I guess I've never been in a situation where I absolutely, 100 percent couldn't get up. I just feel this... this really strong urge to move, and it's so uncomfortable to fight it. I don't know if I could force myself to stay seated if my job depended on it. I just haven't tried to push it that far.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, would you say there's an uncomfortable feeling inside—like an inner agitation or restlessness—that makes you want to move, or is it more that you just don't feel like sitting and your body naturally wants to be active?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I guess I haven't really paid attention to whether it's an inner agitation or just my body wanting to be active. It's hard to separate the two. I just... I just know that sitting still starts to feel wrong after a while, so I get up. I couldn't really say which one of those it is.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting still and feel that urge to get up, would you describe the feeling as actively uncomfortable or distressing—something that bothers you and makes you want to escape it—or is it more that sitting just doesn't feel natural to you and moving feels better?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to describe it, honestly. It's not like... it's not like a sharp pain or something that actively hurts. But it's also not just that moving feels better. I guess I haven't really thought about whether it's distressing or just unnatural. It's just... I get this feeling, and I have to move. I don't know if I can put it into either of those categories.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting and get that urge to move, does it feel like there's something inside you that's bothering you or making you uncomfortable—like you need to escape that feeling by moving—or does it feel more like your body just naturally wants to be in motion, without any sense that sitting is actually bothering you?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know how else to explain it. I feel like you're asking the same question again, and I just don't have a clear answer for it. I haven't analyzed it that deeply. I just sit, I get the urge to move, and I get up. I don't know if it's an inner bother or just my body wanting motion. It's just what happens.\n\nDoctor: If you were completely paralyzed from the neck down but still awake and aware, would you feel a strong inner discomfort or agitation that makes you want to move—even though you couldn't—or would you just feel fine sitting still since your body wouldn't be able to move anyway?\n\nPatient: I... wow, that's an extreme example. I honestly don't know how I'd feel in that situation. I've never been paralyzed, so I can't really guess if I'd feel agitated inside or if I'd just be fine. I mean, my brain would probably still be going a million miles an hour, but as for the physical urge to move? I just don't know. I can't really imagine what that would feel like.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to stand up and pace, if you were forced to stay seated for 10 minutes, would you feel anxious, irritable, or emotionally distressed—like something is wrong or bothering you—or would you just be bored or restless without that emotional upset?\n\nPatient: I think... if I had to sit there for ten minutes and absolutely couldn't get up, I’d probably just feel really restless. Like, my leg would start bouncing, I'd be shifting around in the chair, maybe tapping my fingers. I don't think I'd feel anxious or emotionally upset, like something was terribly wrong. I'd just be really antsy and bored, waiting for the ten minutes to be up so I could finally move.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting and get that urge to move, if you compare it to times when you're bored or waiting in line, is the feeling the same kind of restlessness, or does this urge to move feel like something different—something more internal or harder to ignore?\n\nPatient: I think it's just the same kind of restlessness. Like if I'm stuck waiting in line at the grocery store, I'm shifting my weight, looking around, maybe checking my phone, just wanting things to move along. When I'm sitting and get the urge to get up, it feels pretty much like that. It doesn't feel like some deep, internal... thing that's totally different. It's just that same basic feeling of wanting to be doing something else, or just wanting to be in motion.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on something—like reading a case file or listening to someone speak—do you find your mind wandering or getting distracted easily, or are you able to concentrate on what you're doing even though your body wants to move around?\n\nPatient: I can usually concentrate pretty well. I mean, my body might be doing its own thing—like I said, my leg bouncing or me shifting in my seat—but my mind is on the job. If I'm reading a file or listening to a client, I'm focused on what they're saying or what's on the page. I don't really have a problem with my mind wandering off. It's more just the physical need to be moving while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can usually concentrate well on your work. But when you're reading case files or listening to someone speak, do you ever find it hard to keep your attention on what they're saying, or do you sometimes miss details because your mind drifted to something else?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I was just saying, I don't really have an issue with my mind drifting off. In my line of work, missing details isn't really an option anyway. So, no, I don't find it hard to keep my attention on what someone is saying or what I'm reading. I stay focused on the information, it's just my body that wants to keep moving while I do it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at your book club or in tutoring sessions, do you ever have trouble keeping up with what's being discussed because your mind wanders, or do you follow along fine even though your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I follow along fine. It's the same thing as with work—I don't really have trouble keeping up with the discussion. My mind stays on the book or whatever we're talking about in the tutoring session. I might be shifting around in my chair or tapping my foot, but I'm paying attention to what everyone is saying. I don't really space out or miss things.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that's not very interesting to you—like waiting in a doctor's office or sitting through a boring meeting—do you find it harder to pay attention, or do you still focus just fine even if your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I still focus fine. Even if it's boring, I don't really have a problem paying attention to what's going on. I mean, I might be annoyed that I'm stuck waiting or sitting through something dull, and I'll definitely be fidgeting or wanting to get up and leave, but my mind doesn't wander off. I'm still present and aware of what's happening around me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's more just the physical need to be moving while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you can usually concentrate well on your work. I stay focused on the information, it's just my body that wants to keep moving while I do it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at your book club or in tutoring sessions, do you ever have trouble keeping up with what's being discussed because your mind wanders, or do you follow along fine even though your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I follow along fine. It's the same thing as with work—I don't really have trouble keeping up with the discussion. My mind stays on the book or whatever we're talking about in the tutoring session. I might be shifting around in my chair or tapping my foot, but I'm paying attention to what everyone is saying. I don't really space out or miss things.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something that's not very interesting to you—like waiting in a doctor's office or sitting through a boring meeting—do you find it harder to pay attention, or do you still focus just fine even if your body is restless?\n\nPatient: No, I still focus fine. Even if it's boring, I don't really have a problem paying attention to what's going on. I'm still present and aware of what's happening around me.\n\nDoctor: When you were a kid in elementary school or middle school, do you remember being restless or fidgety back then, or is this something that only really started happening as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's always been this way.
